A compiler failure produces error logs; passing tests produce exit codes and test results. The runtime’s audit log records what happened to every proposal. A rejected proposal leaves external state untouched, whereas a network call whose reply is lost leaves its outcome unknown, because the remote service may have applied the change before the connection dropped. The runtime therefore marks timed-out or truncated runs as unresolved rather than treating silence as success.
  6. Assess against contract: The runtime checks the updated task state against the contract’s completion criteria. Depending on the result, it can start another turn, wait on background jobs, ask the user for clarification, stop the trajectory as unresolved, or accept the deliverable. That decision rests on external evidence and remaining budgets rather than the model’s own report.

These six phases mark boundaries of responsibility rather than a rigid synchronous schedule. In production, authorized tools may run asynchronously against remote services, and a model call may propose a final answer instead of a tool call. Implementations can overlap independent work, but causality fixes the order of decisions: a runtime cannot authorize an action from observations that arrive after dispatch, and it cannot tell whether an external effect succeeded from the proposal alone. Figure 7 draws the phases as a U in which only an authorized request drops from validation to the tool. Its dashed paths carry the exceptions. A rejected request returns to the observation history as error feedback without touching any tool, approvals and background jobs pause the turn, and assessment at the end of the turn either starts the next turn, accepts the deliverable, or stops without acceptance.

The lifecycle also gives each piece of evidence its own record, which keeps the bounded claims of section 1.5.1 from merging into one. Phase 4 records that a patch modified a file. Phase 5 records that compilation succeeded or that a named test passed on the schedules it exercised. Only phase 6 asks whether those records together meet the contract’s completion criteria. A runtime that logged one undifferentiated stream could not tell intermediate execution from verified resolution.

Whether these components run inside one local process or across services in a cluster, the core rule stays the same: authority is separate from generation. Permissions come from the runtime’s grant, completion criteria come from the task contract, and execution records come from tool observations, never from model text, unverified tool replies, or the agent’s own completion tokens.

In practice, running a test suite often takes much longer than generating the patch that triggered it. During that wait, the task stays active even though model inference is idle. The runtime must keep enough context to process the test results when they return. Holding serving state in high-bandwidth accelerator memory throughout a long tool wait, however, wastes scarce capacity, while evicting the key-value cache too early forces expensive recomputation on the next turn. Separating the lifetime of a task from the lifetime of its serving memory is therefore a central systems problem. Holding accelerator memory idle during slow external operations is the tool-wait problem; Paused-State Policies prices the trade-offs of keeping, offloading, or evicting that state.

The same distinction applies to context history. Keeping full diagnostic logs in durable storage for later audits does not mean resending every compiler warning in every prompt. Inference latency, accelerator memory, tool duration, and dollar cost pull in different directions when the runtime decides what to keep, recompute, or summarize. Giving every data record a clear owner and lifetime lets the runtime manage caches and context deliberately, and the same division of responsibilities organizes the rest of the book.

1.8 Book Organization

Improving a model’s proposals does not grant it permission to run a shell command, and sandboxing a tool call does not stop a fifty-turn trajectory from drifting off course or losing its state on a crash. Because none of the layers of the stochastic computer map (figure 3) can do another layer’s job, the parts of this book follow those layers in dependency order. Each part builds on the guarantees beneath it and closes one exposure before the pieces assemble into a complete runtime.

1.8.1 The causal order of the parts

That dependency order starts with a single model call, because every later guarantee depends on what one invocation reads, returns, and costs. Once the runtime chains calls across turns, it must first bound the growing state in context and memory, then contain the authority of tool calls, and finally supervise the multi-turn horizon across crashes, retries, and evaluation. Only after the runtime produces verified records of what happened can an engineering team train on those trajectories or scale across multiple agents. Table 4 maps this dependency chain onto the seven parts of the book.

Within the parts, the chapters follow the same causal order. In Part I, The Foundation Model fixes the contract around a single model call (what a request supplies, how a response reports completion, and what its input and output cost). Test-Time Compute decides when another token, candidate, check, or call is worth its price. Part II addresses state. Context Engineering selects the working set each call receives. KV Cache Management prices the attention state that context leaves in accelerator memory and decides whether to keep, move, or drop it while an agent waits on a tool. Long-Term Memory keeps knowledge across sessions as claims that carry their provenance. Part III addresses authority. Tool Calling places the runtime between a proposed call and its effect; Agent Sandboxes enforces the task’s limits beneath the executing code; and Agent Security defends against inputs that turn legitimate permissions toward an attacker’s ends.

Part IV builds the runtime that governs the horizon. The Agent Harness keeps the decision to continue outside the model, driving the loop under budgets and approval gates. Durable Execution records each intended action before dispatching it, so that a successor can resume after a crash without repeating an effect unsafely. Because restoring a snapshot does not undo an effect on an external system, Failure Recovery decides among retry, repair, compensation, and escalation by the cost still ahead, and halts trajectories that make no progress. Agent Evaluation establishes what evidence supports a claim about a configuration, and Operating Agents carries that evidence into live work through traces, postmortems, and staged releases.

Part V changes the model without expanding its exposure. Trajectory Curation decides whether a failure belongs in the weights at all and which records serve which learner. Trajectory Fine-Tuning trains on the responses curation selects and releases an update only when closed-loop acceptance improves. Reinforcement Learning from Verifiable Rewards replaces demonstrations with a programmatic check and defends that check against an optimizer that searches it for gaps. Part VI examines how scale multiplies all three exposures. Multi-Agent Coordination asks when splitting a task across workers beats a single controller with the same tools and budget, and Agent Economics measures cost per accepted task over a cohort and prices each lever the runtime controls against it. Finally, Conclusion follows one incident remediation, a configuration rollback whose acknowledgment never arrives, across every boundary the book builds.

Each part opens with the systems principles it develops. Each principle is stated formally where it first appears and cited wherever later components depend on its guarantees, beginning with the invariant closure principle (1).

↪ The full table of foundational systems principles, with each principle’s core statement and what it predicts, is developed in The Principles of the Book.

The same ordering of parts also gives a practical order for fixing production failures, because the parts run from changes in what the model reads to changes in the model’s weights. Changing what a model sees in its context is cheap, fast, and easy to roll back. Retraining or fine-tuning model weights is the most expensive and least reversible fix, and it risks hurting performance on tasks the failure never touched. Fixes to schema constraints, tool interfaces, and runtime checks sit between those two ends, ordered by cost and reversibility. That sequence is a sensible default rather than a rigid ladder that every incident must climb step by step.

↪ The intervention ladder, which orders remedies from context assembly through tool schemas and runtime controls to fine-tuning and reinforcement learning by cost and reversibility, is developed in The intervention ladder.

This dependency order is also why evaluation comes before offline learning in this book. A trace can record an action that was allowed and ran cleanly yet failed to advance the task; conversely, an accepted run often contains an earlier failed attempt that showed the agent how to recover. Offline learning methods need those distinctions so they train on genuine recovery rather than brittle luck. For the same reason, multi-agent coordination comes only after a complete single-agent runtime is in place: teams should compare the coordination cost, latency, and variance of multiple agents against a well-engineered single-agent baseline rather than a fragile while-loop.

This sequence is a teaching order rather than a mandate to deploy every mechanism on every workload. A production system needs only the safeguards its exposures demand, and a bounded, read-only query needs far less machinery than an agent authorized to modify live database clusters. The conclusion applies the full framework to the remediation agent (lighthouse 1.3), from an automated latency alert through an operator-approved configuration rollback to verified recovery.

1.8.2 Reading paths by role

An infrastructure engineer sizing KV-cache pools, a platform engineer writing tool schemas, and a post-training researcher curating trajectories all touch different parts of an agent stack, yet a failure in one layer routinely shows up as a symptom in another. To keep those cross-layer dependencies clear without forcing every reader to know accelerator microarchitecture in advance, the main sequence assumes familiarity with foundation-model prompting, autoregressive token generation, systems programming, processes, virtual memory, file systems, networking, remote procedure calls, and basic probability. Beyond those basics, the book is self-contained: agent-specific concepts are defined where they first appear, and the appendices hold the serving-cost and mathematical derivations.

↪ Serving cost derivations for model invocations are developed in Inference Cost Foundations.

Readers with a specific role can weight their focus while keeping this dependency order intact. Infrastructure engineers will lean on model serving contracts, KV-cache memory management, sandboxed execution, durability guarantees, and fleet economics. Platform and application engineers will focus on context engineering, tool interfaces, security containment, the agent harness, failure recovery, and evaluation benchmarks. Post-training researchers will concentrate on test-time compute, trajectory curation, fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards. Every path starts with the task model in this chapter and finishes with the end-to-end trajectory in the conclusion.

1.8.3 The book’s three questions

Without a shared cost metric, teams add autonomous loops where a fixed script would win. Without an exposure model, they overbuild sandboxes for read-only tasks or under-protect production databases. Without independent checks, they mistake a fluent completion message for a finished job. Every chapter that follows therefore answers three questions:

  1. When does a model-directed loop justify its cost over a fixed workflow? Cost per accepted task decides this trade-off. Each chapter develops mechanisms that either raise the probability of acceptance or cut the spend on failed attempts.
  2. What runtime machinery must surround the model? The H·S·A exposure space sizes it. Parts II through IV build the mechanisms that manage context across memory tiers, bound delegated authority against untrusted input, and supervise long horizons.
  3. How do operators know that an autonomous task actually succeeded? Invariant closure governs the answer. A model’s fluent self-report cannot replace external checks; acceptance rests on evidence gathered independently from the environment and judged against a contract adopted before the work begins.

↪ Answers to the three questions, tested with the complete architecture assembled, are developed in Answers to the Three Questions.

1.9 Fallacies and Pitfalls

These three questions frame every mechanism in the book, and they also expose the common traps that appear when teams build or measure agents without them. A patch can compile cleanly without resolving the underlying defect, just as an agent can consume its turn budget without producing fresh evidence. Autonomous execution fails when system designers mistake churn inside the loop for genuine task completion.

Fallacy: A larger context window removes the need to manage task state.

Expanding a context window increases how many tokens a single call can read, but it does not settle which state is current. In an append-only trajectory, stale observations and obsolete file edits pile up alongside current versions. Consider the software repair harness under a 30-turn budget. Starting from an initial prompt of 8,000 tokens and appending 1,500 tokens per turn, the final model call reads 51,500 tokens. This uses only 40 percent of a 128,000-token window, so buffer capacity never forces eviction. Yet without active state pruning, superseded revisions of edited files sit right beside their latest patches. Repeatedly resending earlier turns forces the system to read 892,500 cumulative input tokens, 17.3× the final context size. Even when an entire history fits in memory, raw capacity guarantees neither relevance nor freshness. The runtime must assemble the working context explicitly and mark which state is authoritative rather than leaving the model to untangle conflicting snapshots.

↪ The quadratic input-token cost of resending an append-only history is developed in How multi-turn history drives up input cost.

Pitfall: Appending every failure and retrying until the model reports progress.

Independent retries can improve acceptance, whereas retries that repeat the same failure provide no gain. With a per-attempt acceptance rate of 40 percent, a budget of \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) = 5 independent attempts succeeds with probability \(1-(1-\AcceptanceProbability)^{\IndependentAttemptCount}\), here 92 percent. When an agent appends every failure trace into its prompt, however, the model tends to fixate on its earlier bad assumptions. If every retry repeats the same mistake, 5 attempts succeed no more often than the first attempt while spending 5 times the budget. A well-structured runtime keeps the full diagnostic history in an audit log and feeds the model only the distilled evidence needed for the next decision, under hard retry limits. Recovering from repeated failure requires a new hypothesis, a state change, or fresh evidence from the environment before another repair turn. Alternatively, a declared resampling policy can achieve genuinely independent retries by drawing fresh candidates from the clean starting prompt under a finite budget and independent acceptance checks, though another sample on its own is not evidence of progress. When resetting the prompt for a fresh attempt, the runtime must also roll back the workspace, because truncating or rewriting the working context changes only what the model reads while external effects that have already run persist on disk.

Fallacy: A coherent model report establishes that the task is complete.

A model’s natural-language claim that it has fixed an issue is an unverified hypothesis, not proof of completion. On the closure evidence levels of section 1.5.2 it sits at the bottom, below even a compiler check, because a fail-plausible run produces the same confident report as a correct one. The horizon notebook (1.1) shows the size of the error. Under its assumptions, an unchecked 100-turn repair is correct in only 36.6 percent of runs. If every run ends with a confident completion message, a runtime that trusts that message accepts all of them, and most of its acceptances are wrong. The runtime must instead check the workspace against an explicit evidence standard (sealed tests, static analysis, and the stress runs a concurrency fix requires) and record both the verification artifacts and any remaining gaps.

Pitfall: Expressing an authority limit only as an instruction to the model.

Telling an agent in its prompt to modify files only within a project directory expresses intent, but it provides no protection against model mistakes or prompt injections hidden in untrusted source files. If the host environment exposes raw execution primitives, an agent can be steered into leaking secrets or modifying infrastructure. Read-only file access is not safe on its own either: an agent that reads an API secret from an environment variable and sends it in an outbound network request leaks data without touching a single local file. The execution boundary, not the prompt, must enforce permissions. Runtimes isolate agent processes with operating-system primitives (containers, isolated network namespaces, short-lived credentials, and scoped tool interfaces) that block unauthorized actions regardless of what the model asks to run.

Fallacy: Environmental variation always requires a model-directed loop.

Calling a foundation model to choose the next step after a predictable unit-test failure wastes time and tokens when the fix is a known fallback or a fixed retry sequence. Traditional software workflows handle most routine variation with deterministic control flow: branches, parameterized retries, state machines, and exponential backoffs. Model-directed decisions earn their keep only when choosing the next action requires interpreting open-ended evidence that fixed rules cannot parse, such as combining compiler and test diagnostics to edit several files. Engineers should benchmark agent loops against deterministic workflows under the same acceptance criteria; a loop is worth its latency, token spend, and variance only when it lowers the cost per accepted task.

Pitfall: Charging only the successful final trajectory to the accepted result.

Autonomous task completion frequently requires multiple attempts before a candidate patch satisfies the acceptance checks. Every abandoned branch, failed test run, and discarded attempt consumes compute and wall-clock time, even if its code never merges into the final repository. With a per-attempt acceptance rate of 40 percent, accounting only for the winning trajectory yields an apparent cost of $2.10 per repair, whereas the true expected cost is $5.25, a 2.5× understatement. Complete system accounting must also capture sandbox provisioning, tool execution overhead, and the cost of human triaging. Measured over a cohort, the numerator charges every initiated task, accepted or not, while the denominator counts only tasks accepted under the stated evidence standard. That cohort form, the measured counterpart to equation 2, is developed in Cost per Accepted Task.

Each of these traps comes from mistaking a property of a single model call for a guarantee across the task. A working runtime keeps state current, enforces permissions in the execution layer rather than the prompt, and gates acceptance on independent evidence, judging the system by the cost of its verified outcomes rather than the fluency of its turns.

1.10 Summary

A foundation model generates candidate reasoning traces and actions, yet it cannot verify its own output or safely modify external systems. Four instruments govern the design of the runtime that surrounds it. The stochastic computer map compares an agent to a classical computer and locates where the analogy breaks: because the model only proposes, a deterministic runtime must manage persistent state, enforce permissions, and close the loop. The H·S·A exposure space sizes that runtime along three dimensions: execution horizon (\(\TurnHorizon\)), accumulated state (\(\StateExposureLevel_0\) to \(\StateExposureLevel_3\)), and delegated authority (\(\AuthorityExposureLevel_0\) to \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_3\)). Invariant closure then pairs hard execution boundaries below the model with independent, artifact-based acceptance checks above it.

Two economic relations price those choices. Under the independent-step model without recovery, unchecked multi-turn runs compound step errors exponentially, so catching and repairing errors early shortens the effective horizon over which mistakes accumulate. Measuring performance by cost per accepted task charges every wasted model call, sandbox run, and human review across failed attempts to the verified deliverables that pass. As the repository repair walkthrough and the four lighthouse systems show, runtime machinery should scale with exposure: an agent needs only the safeguards that its horizon, state, and authority demand.

Table 5 summarizes the quantitative relations used throughout this chapter to evaluate reliability and cost across an execution horizon, where \(\PerStepErrorRate\) is the per-step error rate, \(\TurnHorizon\) is the horizon length in turns, and \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) is the allocated retry budget. Cost per Accepted Task measures cost per accepted task over a whole cohort of initiated tasks.

Key Takeaways: Agency is a property of the system, not the model
  • Stochastic proposals need a closing runtime: An agent resembles a classical computer, but its foundation model only proposes. It generates candidate tokens and actions, leaving the validation of correctness and actual execution to external software. The surrounding runtime must enforce permissions, preserve state across steps, and govern the full trajectory through failures and retries.
  • Task exposure dictates architectural obligations: The H·S·A dimensions (horizon length, accumulated state, and delegated authority) pinpoint where an autonomous workflow requires protective sandboxes, durable checkpoints, human intervention, and verifiable evidence. System mechanisms should scale with this exposure profile; tasks with short horizons and constrained authority do not require the heavyweight control loops of high-exposure systems.
  • Runtime enforcement and empirical evidence serve distinct roles: Structural boundaries enforce hard constraints on permitted actions, preventing catastrophic side effects regardless of model behavior. By contrast, verification checks produce empirical evidence only for the specific properties they measure. Task acceptance requires evaluating this evidence against a defined contract. Passing tests support claims only within their measured coverage; an eloquent report alone supplies no independent evidence.
  • Two numbers price every mechanism, autonomy included: Under the independent-step model without recovery, unchecked task success decays exponentially with horizon length, so detecting and repairing errors before later turns depend on them can improve reliability without changing the model. Meanwhile, the cost per accepted task amortizes all wasted inference, compute infrastructure, and human oversight across verified deliverables. An autonomous loop justifies its operational complexity only when it reduces this net cost under the task contract.

1.11 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The closed-loop architecture at the core of agentic systems predates modern machine learning by decades. In founding cybernetics, Norbert Wiener established feedback as the organizing principle of control: a system observes its environment, measures the discrepancy between current state and a target goal, and applies corrective actions (Wiener 1948). Writing a decade later, Wiener warned of the operational hazard inherent in rapid feedback: when an automated machine acts faster than a human operator can intervene, it must be constrained to the exact purpose intended (Wiener 1960). Artificial intelligence adopted this structure through the rational agent framework, defining an agent by its continuous cycle of perception and environment interaction (Russell and Norvig 2020). Reinforcement learning subsequently formalized that cycle, treating the iterative trajectory of states, actions, rewards, and observations as the central object of learning (Sutton and Barto 2018).

Russell, Stuart, and Peter Norvig. 2020. Artificial Intelligence: A Modern Approach. 4th ed. Pearson.

What transformed with foundation models was the capability of the component driving the loop. Rather than relying on specialized heuristics or hand-crafted policies, a general-purpose model pretrained across diverse data distributions can now synthesize reasoning traces and emit concrete actions in text and code (Bommasani et al. 2021). The ReAct architecture demonstrated that interleaving model-generated reasoning with tool execution feedback allows language models to operate effectively in open software environments (Yao et al. 2023). Real-world software engineering benchmarks crystallized this shift, demonstrating that an agent’s capability must be measured not by fluency on static prompts, but by whether its proposed patches pass unseen repository test suites (Jimenez et al. 2024).

The scaffolding surrounding the model builds on classical systems research addressing unreliable components. John von Neumann first investigated how to synthesize dependable computational systems from failure-prone parts, demonstrating that modular redundancy, majority voting, and periodic state restoration can bound compound failure rates (Neumann 1956). Subsequent distributed systems research formalized the fault assumptions that supervisors must accommodate. These range from fail-stop models, where damaged components halt detectably (Schlichting and Schneider 1983), to Byzantine failure models, where components behave arbitrarily or deceptively (Lamport et al. 1982). The modern dependability taxonomy codified the causal sequence linking latent faults, runtime errors, and observable system failures, drawing an essential distinction between errors that trigger overt alarms and those that silently corrupt downstream execution (Avizienis et al. 2004).

Dijkstra, Edsger W. 1970. “Structured Programming.” Software Engineering Techniques: Report on a Conference Sponsored by the NATO Science Committee, 84–87.

Because foundation models generate probabilistic outputs, verification cannot be offloaded to the generator itself. The end-to-end argument in system design established that complete correctness checks cannot be delegated to intermediate subcomponents; they must ultimately reside at the endpoint that understands application-level task requirements (Saltzer et al. 1984). At the same time, Dijkstra’s foundational observation that software testing demonstrates the presence of bugs rather than their absence bounds the certainty that any validation suite can provide (Dijkstra 1970).

Managing delegation and authority safely relies on principles developed across operating systems and distributed transaction design. Capability-based addressing, introduced by Dennis and Van Horn, bound access rights directly to unforgeable, fine-grained object references rather than ambient process identities (Dennis and Van Horn 1966). Saltzer and Schroeder codified these patterns into enduring design principles for computer protection, emphasizing least privilege, complete mediation of access requests, and economy of mechanism (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975). Hardy later uncovered the hazard of ambient authority: in a confused deputy attack, an unprivileged client manipulates a privileged utility into misusing permissions granted by its environment rather than by the specific caller (Hardy 1988).

Dennis, Jack B., and Earl C. Van Horn. 1966. “Programming Semantics for Multiprogrammed Computations.” Communications of the ACM 9 (3): 143–55. https://doi.org/10.1145/365230.365252.
Greshake, Kai, Sahar Abdelnabi, Shailesh Mishra, Christoph Endres, Thorsten Holz, and Mario Fritz. 2023. “Not What You’ve Signed up for: Compromising Real-World LLM-Integrated Applications with Indirect Prompt Injection.” Proceedings of the 16th ACM Workshop on Artificial Intelligence and Security, 79–90. https://doi.org/10.1145/3605764.3623985.

Extended autonomous execution also confronts the reality that multi-step external workflows cannot be rolled back atomically. Garcia-Molina and Salem developed sagas to manage long-running transactions, coordinating forward progress through predefined compensating actions when operations fail midway (Garcia-Molina and Salem 1987). At interface boundaries, Meyer’s design by contract established that modular software reliability depends on explicit, executable preconditions, postconditions, and invariants (Meyer 1992). Autonomous agents reopen each of these classical security and reliability challenges simultaneously. Because language models interleave data interpretation with control flow, external data cannot be assumed benign. Greshake et al. (2023) demonstrated that indirect prompt injection turns untrusted external content into instructions the model may follow. The result shows that for an agent guided by natural language, read access is itself an exposure, because what the agent reads can steer every action it proposes, even though only the runtime can grant those actions.

  • The Protection of Information in Computer Systems (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975). The classic formulation of protection mechanisms for computer systems, including least privilege, complete mediation, and fail-safe defaults. This paper provides the conceptual foundation for modern tool authorization, sandboxing, and privilege boundaries in agent architectures.
  • End-to-End Arguments in System Design (Saltzer et al. 1984). The foundational rationale for placing validation checks at the application endpoints that maintain task semantics. In agent engineering, it clarifies why intermediate generation fluency cannot certify end-to-end task completion.
  • Basic Concepts and Taxonomy of Dependable and Secure Computing (Avizienis et al. 2004). The standard taxonomy distinguishing faults, errors, and failures across computing systems. It provides the disciplined vocabulary needed to diagnose agent failure modes, including the fail-plausible errors characteristic of foundation models.
  • Principles of Computer System Design (Saltzer and Kaashoek 2009). A comprehensive textbook on system decomposition, atomicity, virtualization, and protection. It supplies the essential systems foundation for building resilient agent orchestrators, runtimes, and tool harnesses.
  • Reinforcement Learning: An Introduction (Sutton and Barto 2018). The definitive treatise on sequential decision-making in dynamic environments. It provides the formal vocabulary of states, actions, rewards, and environment trajectories that underpins the execution model in this chapter.
  • ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models (Yao et al. 2023). The seminal paper demonstrating that interleaving reasoning traces with tool invocations enables language models to solve multi-step problems. Reading it alongside this chapter highlights the boundary between the model’s proposed actions and the external system responsible for executing and verifying them.
Saltzer, Jerome H., David P. Reed, and David D. Clark. 1984. “End-to-End Arguments in System Design.” ACM Transactions on Computer Systems 2 (4): 277–88. https://doi.org/10.1145/357401.357402.
Saltzer, Jerome H., and M. Frans Kaashoek. 2009. Principles of Computer System Design: An Introduction. Morgan Kaufmann.
Sutton, Richard S., and Andrew G. Barto. 2018. Reinforcement Learning: An Introduction. 2nd ed. MIT Press.
Yao, Shunyu, Jeffrey Zhao, Dian Yu, Nan Du, Izhak Shafran, Karthik Narasimhan, and Yuan Cao. 2023. “ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

1.12 Exercises

All quantities below are hypothetical problem givens, chosen to be round, and describe no particular model, provider, or deployment. Unless a problem says otherwise, assume step errors are independent, each attempt costs the same in expectation, and “accepted” means accepted under a fixed task contract.

1. How far can an unchecked repair run? (Basic). A repair agent takes steps that are each correct with probability 0.98, and no step is checked before the next one builds on it.

  1. Using equation 1, compute task success at horizons of 20 and 60 turns.
  2. Find the per-step error rate that would hold the 60-turn task at 80 percent success, and how many times lower it is than the current error rate. Confirm the answer with the small-\(\PerStepErrorRate\) form \(\PerStepErrorRate \TurnHorizon \approx -\ln(0.8)\).
  3. Instead of a better model, the runtime inserts a check after every 10 turns that detects any incorrect step in that segment and reruns the segment from the preceding checkpoint. Assuming the check is perfect and free, compute the expected number of turns to finish the 60-turn task, and compare it with rerunning the whole unchecked trajectory until it succeeds. State which lever to pull first and the property of the check on which the answer depends.

2. A token discount or a better context? (Basic). An attempt at a repair task spends $1.80 on model tokens and $1.20 on the sandbox, tests, and review, and 25 percent of attempts are accepted. The runtime retries until acceptance.

  1. Compute the expected number of attempts and the cost per accepted task from equation 2.
  2. A provider offers a 40 percent token discount; alternatively, a context change raises acceptance to 30 percent at the original attempt cost. Compute the cost per accepted task under each, and under both together.
  3. Find the acceptance probability that matches the discount. Explain why the comparison comes out differently from the chapter’s notebook (1.2), naming the quantity that decides which lever wins.

3. Placing a workload in H·S·A (Basic). A log-triage agent runs about 15 turns per incident and spends $0.50 on tokens per task. Consider three versions of its grant: (i) it reads logs and writes a summary to a scratch file; (ii) it also opens tickets in a shared tracker, and any ticket can be closed again; (iii) it also rolls back a production deployment, and a wrong rollback causes an outage costing $2,000 that cannot be undone. In version (iii), 2 percent of rollbacks are wrong.

  1. Place each version on the state and authority axes of figure 4, justifying each level by the consequence of the operation rather than its name.
  2. For version (iii), compute the expected loss per task from wrong rollbacks and express it as a multiple of the token cost.
  3. A human approval step costs $5 per rollback and stops 90 percent of wrong ones. Compute the expected cost per task with approval, and state which exposure binds this workload.
  4. Specify all five task-contract fields for version (iii): the useful incident-triage goal and its negative scope, the deployment baseline and execution environment, the permitted actions and approval boundary, bounded diagnostic observations, and independent completion criteria. Name one limitation of the evidence that would require an unresolved outcome or further review.

4. Paying for checks inside the horizon (Intermediate). A repair task runs 50 turns with per-step error 0.02. Each turn costs $0.04 in tokens, and each attempt pays $1.00 to prepare a fresh sandbox. Under policy A, only a sealed test at the end checks the result; it accepts an attempt exactly when every step was correct, and a rejected attempt restarts from scratch. Under policy B, the same sandbox is kept and a check costing $0.10 runs after every 10 turns, detecting any incorrect step in that segment, which is then rerun from the last checkpoint.

  1. Compute the acceptance probability and cost per accepted task under policy A.
  2. Compute the expected cost per accepted task under policy B, and the expected number of turns under each policy.
  3. Find the per-check cost at which policy B stops paying. Then critique the assumption that makes B look so strong: what happens to the calculation if the intermediate check is a visible test the agent can fit its patch to? Specify independent final evidence and concurrency coverage appropriate to this repair, using section 1.5.2, and explain why sealing a sequential test alone would not establish that the race condition is fixed.

5. When the loss is not in the tokens (Intermediate; second lighthouse). The remediation agent (lighthouse 1.3) spends $2 on tokens per attempt. Each attempt ends by proposing one configuration change that is correct with probability 0.95; a wrong change that commits costs $20,000 in total. A human approval step costs $15 of on-call time and delays restoration by 6 minutes, during which the ongoing incident costs $50 per minute. Approval passes every correct change and rejects 90 percent of wrong ones; a rejected proposal starts a new attempt, which needs approval again.

  1. Without approval, every proposal commits. Compute the expected cost per task, including expected loss.
  2. With approval, compute the probability that an attempt ends in a commit, the expected attempts per task, the expected attempt spend, the probability the committed change is wrong, and the total expected cost per task.
  3. Find the incident cost per minute above which approval stops paying.
  4. For the repair agent, the chapter’s levers were acceptance and horizon. Show numerically why halving the token price is irrelevant here, and name the binding constraint of this lighthouse and the H·S·A level that makes it bind.

6. Workflow, loop, or both? (Intermediate). A fixed workflow attempt for a support query costs $0.60; a model-directed loop attempt costs $1.80. Half the queries are routine, where the workflow is accepted with probability 0.55 and the loop with 0.80. The other half are novel, where the workflow is accepted with probability 0.05 and the loop with 0.70. Each query retries its chosen architecture until acceptance.

  1. Compute the cost per accepted task for each architecture on each query class, and the mean over the population if every query uses the workflow, and if every query uses the loop.
  2. A perfect router sends routine queries to the workflow and novel ones to the loop. Compute the mean cost per accepted query.
  3. A real router costs $0.05 per query and sends a fraction \(\MisroutedQueryFraction\) of each class to the wrong architecture. Compute the mean cost at \(\MisroutedQueryFraction = 0.05\) and find the \(\MisroutedQueryFraction\) at which routing stops beating the loop alone. What does this imply about where to spend engineering effort, and what must be held fixed for the comparison to be valid?

7. Retries that repeat one mistake (Advanced). Each attempt at a repair task costs $2.00, and the retry budget allows 4 attempts. Of the tasks, 70 percent are easy, accepted with probability 0.70 per attempt; the rest are hard, and because each retry rereads the transcript and repeats the same mistaken hypothesis, a hard task is accepted with probability only 0.05 per attempt. Assume attempts are independent conditional on the task being easy or hard; the repeated hypothesis lowers the hard-task acceptance rate rather than making every retry share one outcome. Tasks not accepted within the budget end unresolved, and their spend stays in the numerator.

  1. An analyst pools the cohort into one mean acceptance probability and applies the independent-retry formulas. Compute the pooled acceptance, the predicted success within the budget, and the predicted cost per accepted task.
  2. Compute the actual acceptance within the budget, expected spend per task, and measured cost per accepted task under the mixture. By what factor did the pooled model understate the cost?
  3. Redesign the retry. Retries 2 through 4 start from a fresh context carrying only the failed test result, which costs $2.50 per retry and raises a hard task’s retry acceptance to 0.25 while leaving easy tasks unchanged. Compute the new acceptance within the budget and cost per accepted task, and argue whether the redesign should ship. What does the result say about treating \(\AcceptanceProbability\) as a property of the model rather than of the whole attempt?

8. Should the repair agent deploy its own patches? (Advanced). A team produces 200 patches that pass the sealed tests each day. Of these patches, 3 percent are in fact wrong. A wrong patch that reaches production costs $5,000. The team proposes letting the agent deploy directly. Two alternatives are on the table. A reviewer costing $25 per patch catches 80 percent of the wrong patches that tests pass, and waiting for review delays each patch by 4 hours valued at $10 per hour. Alternatively, a canary rollout costing $5 per patch exposes a small slice of traffic and rolls back automatically, bounding the cost of a wrong patch to $500.

  1. Place the direct-deploy proposal and the canary design on the H·S·A authority axis, and explain what changes the level.
  2. Compute the expected cost per patch and per day for direct deploy, review, and canary.
  3. Critique the canary design. Name one class of change for which automatic rollback is not an adequate reversal, state what authority level such a change returns to, and specify the contract change (permitted actions and completion criteria) that should route it back to review.

What’s Next: From task responsibilities to the model call
Of the six phases every turn passes through, only the second belongs to the model, and each of the other five depends on knowing exactly what that phase delivers. When the repair agent asks to inspect the connection pool, the runtime may receive a complete tool request, a request cut off before it is whole, or no response at all, and each outcome requires different handling. The Foundation Model follows that single inspection call from request to bill, establishing what one call reads, how its status reports completion, and what it costs. The single call sits at the origin of the H·S·A exposure space, so this invocation contract becomes the baseline from which every later part measures a departure.
